            "category": "PYQ",
            "single": {
                "question": "Consider the following statements:\n\n**Statement I:** In India, State Governments have no power for making rules for grant of concessions in respect of extraction of minor minerals even though such minerals are located in their territories.\n\n**Statement II:** In India, the Central Government has the power to notify minor minerals under the relevant law.\n\nWhich one of the following is correct in respect of the above statements?",
                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "Statement I: 'State Governments have no power to make rules for minor mineral concessions' — **INCORRECT**. Under Section 15 of the Mines and Minerals (Development and Regulation) Act, 1957, State Governments DO have the power to make rules for regulating the grant of quarry leases, mining leases, and other concessions for minor minerals. In fact, minor minerals are primarily under state jurisdiction. States set royalty rates, grant/refuse mining permits, and regulate extraction of minor minerals like sand, gravel, building stones, etc. ✗\n\nStatement II: 'Central Government has the power to notify minor minerals' — **CORRECT**. Under Section 3(e) of the MMDR Act, the Central Government has the authority to declare/notify which minerals are classified as 'minor minerals' (as opposed to major minerals). Once a mineral is notified as a minor mineral by the Centre, its regulation falls primarily to the state governments. The distinction between major and minor minerals is made by the Central Government through notification. ✓\n\nStatement I is not correct, Statement II is correct. **Answer is (d).**",
                "option_a": "Both Statement I and Statement II are correct and Statement II explains Statement I",
                "option_b": "Both Statement I and Statement II are correct but Statement II does not explain Statement I",
                "option_c": "Statement I is correct but Statement II is not correct",
                "option_d": "Statement I is not correct but Statement II is correct",
                "pyq_year": "2025",
                "marks": 2,
                "negative_marks": 0.66,
                "formatted_question": "Consider the following statements:\n\n**Statement I:** In India, State Governments have no power for making rules for grant of concessions in respect of extraction of minor minerals even though such minerals are located in their territories.\n\n**Statement II:** In India, the Central Government has the power to notify minor minerals under the relevant law.\n\nWhich one of the following is correct in respect of the above statements?",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "Statement I: 'State Governments have no power to make rules for minor mineral concessions' — **INCORRECT**. \n\nUnder **Section 15** of the **Mines and Minerals (Development and Regulation) Act, 1957**, State Governments **DO** have the power to make rules for regulating the grant of quarry leases, mining leases, and other concessions for minor minerals. \n\nIn fact, minor minerals are primarily under state jurisdiction. \n\nStates set royalty rates, grant/refuse mining permits, and regulate extraction of minor minerals like sand, gravel, building stones, etc. \n\nStatement II: 'Central Government has the power to notify minor minerals' — **CORRECT**. \n\nUnder **Section 3(e)** of the **MMDR Act**, the Central Government has the authority to declare/notify which minerals are classified as 'minor minerals' (as opposed to major minerals). \n\nOnce a mineral is notified as a minor mineral by the Centre, its regulation falls primarily to the state governments. \n\nThe distinction between major and minor minerals is made by the Central Government through notification. \n\nStatement I is not correct, Statement II is correct. **Answer is (d).**"

                "question": "Consider the following statements about Lokpal:\n\nI. The power of Lokpal applies to public servants of India, but not to the Indian public servants posted outside India.\nII. The Chairperson or a Member shall not be a Member of the Parliament or a Member of the Legislature of any State or Union Territory, and only the Chief Justice of India, whether incumbent or retired, has to be its Chairperson.\nIII. The Chairperson or a Member shall not be a person of less than forty-five years of age on the date of assuming office as the Chairperson or Member, as the case may be.\nIV. Lokpal cannot inquire into the allegations of corruption against a sitting Prime Minister of India.\n\nWhich of the statements given above is/are correct?",
                "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "Let's check each statement about the Lokpal:\n\n(I) 'Lokpal power doesn't apply to Indian public servants posted outside India' — **INCORRECT**. The Lokpal's jurisdiction extends to all public servants as defined in the Act, regardless of where they are posted. A public servant posted in an Indian embassy abroad, for instance, would still be within Lokpal's jurisdiction. ✗\n\n(II) 'Only the CJI can be Chairperson' — **INCORRECT**. Under the Lokpal Act, the Chairperson can be a person who is or has been a Chief Justice of India OR a judge of the Supreme Court OR an eminent person of impeccable integrity and outstanding ability having special knowledge. It is NOT restricted to only the CJI. ✗\n\n(III) 'Minimum age of 45 years for Chairperson/Member' — **CORRECT**. Section 3(2) of the Lokpal and Lokayuktas Act, 2013 clearly states that no person shall be appointed as Chairperson or Member if they are less than 45 years of age. ✓\n\n(IV) 'Lokpal cannot inquire against a sitting PM' — **INCORRECT**. The Lokpal CAN inquire into allegations against a sitting Prime Minister, but with special safeguards: the full bench must consider the case, the hearing must be in camera (private), and if the Lokpal decides not to proceed, the records cannot be made public. The PM is excluded only for matters related to international relations, external/internal security, public order, and atomic energy. ✗\n\nOnly Statement III is correct. **Answer is (a).**",
                "option_a": "III only",
                "option_b": "II and III",
                "option_c": "I and IV",
                "option_d": "None of the above statements is correct",
                "pyq_year": "2025",
                "marks": 2,
                "negative_marks": 0.66,

                "formatted_answer_explanation": "Let's check each statement about the **Lokpal**:\n\n(I) 'Lokpal power doesn't apply to Indian public servants posted outside India' — **INCORRECT**. \n\nThe Lokpal's jurisdiction extends to all public servants as defined in the Act, regardless of where they are posted. \n\nA public servant posted in an Indian embassy abroad, for instance, would still be within Lokpal's jurisdiction. \n\n✗\n\n(II) 'Only the CJI can be Chairperson' — **INCORRECT**. \n\nUnder the Lokpal Act, the Chairperson can be a person who is or has been a Chief Justice of India OR a judge of the Supreme Court OR an eminent person of impeccable integrity and outstanding ability having special knowledge. \n\nIt is NOT restricted to only the CJI. \n\n✗\n\n(III) 'Minimum age of 45 years for Chairperson/Member' — **CORRECT**. \n\nSection 3(2) of the Lokpal and Lokayuktas Act, **2013** clearly states that no person shall be appointed as Chairperson or Member if they are less than **45 years** of age. \n\n✓\n\n(IV) 'Lokpal cannot inquire against a sitting PM' — **INCORRECT**. \n\nThe Lokpal CAN inquire into allegations against a sitting Prime Minister, but with special safeguards: the full bench must consider the case, the hearing must be in camera (private), and if the Lokpal decides not to proceed, the records cannot be made public. \n\nThe PM is excluded only for matters related to international relations, external/internal security, public order, and atomic energy. \n\n✗\n\nOnly Statement III is correct. **Answer is (a).**"

                "question": "Consider the following statements with regard to pardoning power of the President of India:\n\nI. The exercise of this power by the President can be subjected to limited judicial review.\nII. The President can exercise this power without the advice of the Central Government.\n\nWhich of the statements given above is/are correct?",
                "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "(I) 'President's pardoning power can be subjected to limited judicial review' — **CORRECT**. The Supreme Court has held in several cases (including Maru Ram v. Union of India, 1980 and Epuru Sudhakar v. Government of AP, 2006) that the President's clemency power under Article 72 is NOT absolute and can be judicially reviewed, though the scope of review is limited. Courts can intervene if the decision is: arbitrary or mala fide, taken without applying mind, based on irrelevant considerations, or suffers from non-application of mind. Courts generally don't question the merits of the clemency decision but can check the decision-making process. ✓\n\n(II) 'President can exercise this power without the advice of the Central Government' — **INCORRECT**. Under Article 74(1) of the Constitution, the President acts on the aid and advice of the Council of Ministers headed by the Prime Minister. The pardoning power is NOT a personal/discretionary power of the President — it is exercised on the advice of the government. The President cannot unilaterally grant pardons against the government's recommendation. ✗\n\nOnly Statement I is correct. **Answer is (a).**",
                "option_a": "I only",
                "option_b": "II only",
                "option_c": "Both I and II",
                "option_d": "Neither I nor II",
                "pyq_year": "2025",
                "marks": 2,

                "formatted_answer_explanation": "(I) 'President's pardoning power can be subjected to limited judicial review' — **CORRECT**. \n\nThe Supreme Court has held in several cases (including **Maru Ram v. Government of AP, 2006**) that the President's clemency power under **Article 72** is **NOT** absolute and can be judicially reviewed, though the scope of review is limited. \n\nCourts can intervene if the decision is: \n- arbitrary or mala fide \n- taken without applying mind \n- based on irrelevant considerations \n- suffers from non-application of mind. \n\nCourts generally don't question the merits of the clemency decision but can check the decision-making process. ✓\n\n(II) 'President can exercise this power without the advice of the Central Government' — **INCORRECT**. \n\nUnder **Article 74(1)** of the Constitution, the President acts on the aid and advice of the Council of Ministers headed by the Prime Minister. \n\nThe pardoning power is **NOT** a personal/discretionary power of the President — it is exercised on the advice of the government. \n\nThe President cannot unilaterally grant pardons against the government's recommendation. ✗\n\nOnly Statement I is correct. **Answer is (a).**"

                "question": "Consider the following statements:\n\nI. Panchayats at the intermediate level exist in all States.\nII. To be eligible to be a Member of a Panchayat at the intermediate level, a person should attain the age of thirty years.\nIII. The Chief Minister of a State constitutes a commission to review the financial position of Panchayats at the intermediate levels and to make recommendations regarding the distribution of net proceeds of taxes and duties, leviable by the State, between the State and Panchayats at the intermediate level.\n\nWhich of the statements given above are **not** correct?",
                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "The question asks which statements are NOT correct. Let's check each:\n\n(I) 'Panchayats at the intermediate level exist in all States' — **NOT CORRECT**. Article 243B(2) of the Constitution says that panchayats at the intermediate level need NOT be constituted in states with a population not exceeding 20 lakhs. So small states are exempt, meaning intermediate panchayats don't exist everywhere. ✗\n\n(II) 'Minimum age for intermediate panchayat membership is 30 years' — **NOT CORRECT**. Article 243F(1) states that a person must have attained the age of 21 years (not 30) to be eligible for membership of a panchayat at any level. The 30-year age requirement applies to the Rajya Sabha, not panchayats. ✗\n\n(III) 'Chief Minister constitutes the finance commission for panchayats' — **NOT CORRECT**. Under Article 243I, the GOVERNOR of the State (not the Chief Minister) constitutes a Finance Commission every five years to review the financial position of panchayats and recommend the distribution of taxes and grants. The Finance Commission is a constitutional body appointed by the Governor. ✗\n\nAll three statements are incorrect. **Answer is (d).**",
                "option_a": "I and II only",
                "option_b": "II and III only",
                "option_c": "I and III only",
                "option_d": "I, II and III",
                "pyq_year": "2025",
                "marks": 2,
                "negative_marks": 0.66,
                "formatted_question": "Consider the following statements:\n\nI. Panchayats at the intermediate level exist in all States.\nII. To be eligible to be a Member of a Panchayat at the intermediate level, a person should attain the age of thirty years.\nIII. The Chief Minister of a State constitutes a commission to review the financial position of Panchayats at the intermediate levels and to make recommendations regarding the distribution of net proceeds of taxes and duties, leviable by the State, between the State and Panchayats at the intermediate level.\n\nWhich of the statements given above are **not** correct?",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "The question asks which statements are **NOT CORRECT**. \n\nLet's check each:\n\n(I) 'Panchayats at the intermediate level exist in all States' — **NOT CORRECT**. \n\nArticle 243B(2) of the Constitution says that panchayats at the intermediate level need **NOT** be constituted in states with a population not exceeding **20 lakhs**. \n\nSo small states are exempt, meaning intermediate panchayats don't exist everywhere. \n\n(II) 'Minimum age for intermediate panchayat membership is **30 years**' — **NOT CORRECT**. \n\nArticle 243F(1) states that a person must have attained the age of **21 years** (not **30**) to be eligible for membership of a panchayat at any level. \n\nThe **30-year** age requirement applies to the **Rajya Sabha**, not panchayats. \n\n(III) 'Chief Minister constitutes the finance commission for panchayats' — **NOT CORRECT**. \n\nUnder Article 243I, the **GOVERNOR** of the State (not the Chief Minister) constitutes a Finance Commission every five years to review the financial position of panchayats and recommend the distribution of taxes and grants. \n\nThe Finance Commission is a constitutional body appointed by the Governor. \n\nAll three statements are incorrect. \n\n**Answer is (d).**"

                "question": "With reference to the Indian polity, consider the following statements:\n\nI. The Governor of a State is not answerable to any court for the exercise and performance of the powers and duties of his/her office.\nII. No criminal proceedings shall be instituted or continued against the Governor during his/her term of office.\nIII. Members of a State Legislature are not liable to any proceedings in any court in respect of anything said within the House.\n\nWhich of the statements given above are correct?",
                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "(I) 'Governor not answerable to any court for exercise of official powers' — **CORRECT**. Article 361(1) explicitly states: 'The Governor of a State shall not be answerable to any court for the exercise and performance of the powers and duties of his office or for any act done or purporting to be done by him in the exercise and performance of those powers and duties.' This provides complete immunity from court proceedings for official acts. ✓\n\n(II) 'No criminal proceedings against Governor during term' — **CORRECT**. Article 361(2) states: 'No criminal proceedings whatsoever shall be instituted or continued against the Governor of a State in any court during his term of office.' Additionally, Article 361(3) provides that no civil suit for personal acts can be instituted during tenure without two months' prior notice. ✓\n\n(III) 'State Legislature members not liable for anything said within the House' — **CORRECT**. Article 194(2) provides: 'No member of the Legislature of a State shall be liable to any proceedings in any court in respect of anything said or any vote given by him in the Legislature or any committee thereof.' This is parliamentary privilege — it ensures legislators can speak freely in the House without fear of legal consequences. ✓\n\nAll three statements are correct. **Answer is (d).**",
                "option_a": "I and II only",
                "option_b": "II and III only",
                "option_c": "I and III only",
                "option_d": "I, II and III",
                "pyq_year": "2025",
                "marks": 2,
                "negative_marks": 0.66,

The Governor of a State is not answerable to any court for the exercise and performance of the powers and duties of his/her office.\nII. No criminal proceedings shall be instituted or continued against the Governor during his/her term of office.\nIII. Members of a State Legislature are not liable to any proceedings in any court in respect of anything said within the House.\n\nWhich of the statements given above are correct?",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "(I) 'Governor not answerable to any court for exercise of official powers' — **CORRECT**. \n\nArticle 361(1) explicitly states: \n\n'The Governor of a State shall not be answerable to any court for the exercise and performance of the powers and duties of his office or for any act done or purporting to be done by him in the exercise and performance of those powers and duties.' \n\nThis provides complete immunity from court proceedings for official acts. ✓\n\n(II) 'No criminal proceedings against Governor during term' — **CORRECT**. \n\nArticle 361(2) states: \n\n'No criminal proceedings whatsoever shall be instituted or continued against the Governor of a State in any court during his term of office.' \n\nAdditionally, Article 361(3) provides that no civil suit for personal acts can be instituted during tenure without two months' prior notice. ✓\n\n(III) 'State Legislature members not liable for anything said within the House' — **CORRECT**. \n\nArticle 194(2) provides: \n\n'No member of the Legislature of a State shall be liable to any proceedings in any court in respect of anything said or any vote given by him in the Legislature or any committee thereof.' \n\nThis is parliamentary privilege — it ensures legislators can speak freely in the House without fear of legal consequences. ✓\n\nAll three statements are correct. **Answer is (d).**"

It was established in 1990 on the recommendation of the Sarkaria Commission. ✓ Constitutional body.\n\n(II) National Security Council — **NOT CONSTITUTIONAL**. The NSC was created by an executive order in 1998 under the Vajpayee government.                 "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "(I) 'Constitution explicitly mentions Governor acts in own discretion in certain spheres' — **CORRECT**. Article 163(1) states that there shall be a Council of Ministers to aid and advise the Governor EXCEPT in so far as the Governor is by or under the Constitution required to exercise functions in his discretion. Several provisions require the Governor to act in discretion: reserving bills for Presidential consideration (Article 200), appointing CM when no party has a clear majority, reporting to the President about failure of constitutional machinery in a state (Article 356), and special responsibilities under Article 371 for certain states. ✓\n\n(II) 'President can directly reserve a State bill without Governor forwarding it' — **INCORRECT**. The Governor can give assent, withhold assent, or reserve the bill for Presidential consideration (Article 200). There is NO provision for the President to directly pull up or reserve a State bill on their own — the bill MUST be forwarded by the Governor first. Conditions of the Governor's office\n\nFor a constitutional amendment with respect to which of the above, ratification by the Legislatures of not less than one-half of the States is required before presenting the bill to the President of India for assent?",
                "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "Under Article 368 of the Constitution, certain types of constitutional amendments require ratification by the legislatures of not less than one-half of the States (in addition to the special majority in Parliament). These typically involve matters that affect the federal structure.\n\n(I) List I (Union List) in the Seventh Schedule — **YES**, requires state ratification. Changing the Union List affects the distribution of legislative powers between Centre and States, which is fundamental to federalism. ✓\n\n(II) Extent of executive power of a State — **YES**, requires state ratification. The executive power of a State (Article 162) is co-extensive with its legislative power. Any amendment affecting this directly impacts state autonomy. ✓\n\n(III) Conditions of Governor's office — NO, does NOT require state ratification. The Governor's office conditions (Articles 153-156 — appointment, term, qualifications, etc.) can be amended by the special majority in Parliament alone. The Governor is a Central appointee, and changing their office conditions doesn't alter the fundamental federal balance in the way that changing legislative lists or state executive power does. ✗\n\nI and II require state ratification. **Answer is (a).**",
                "option_a": "I and II only",
                "option_b": "II and III only",
                "option_c": "I and III only",

                "formatted_answer_explanation": "Under Article 368 of the Constitution, certain types of constitutional amendments require ratification by the legislatures of not less than one-half of the States (in addition to the special majority in Parliament). \n\nThese typically involve matters that affect the federal structure.\n\n(I) List I (Union List) in the Seventh Schedule — **YES**, requires state ratification. \n\nChanging the Union List affects the distribution of legislative powers between Centre and States, which is fundamental to federalism. ✓\n\n(II) Extent of executive power of a State — **YES**, requires state ratification. \n\nThe executive power of a State (Article 162) is co-extensive with its legislative power. \n\nAny amendment affecting this directly impacts state autonomy. ✓\n\n(III) Conditions of Governor's office — **NO**, does NOT require state ratification. \n\nThe Governor's office conditions (Articles 153-156 — appointment, term, qualifications, etc.) can be amended by the special majority in Parliament alone. \n\nThe Governor is a Central appointee, and changing their office conditions doesn't alter the fundamental federal balance in the way that changing legislative lists or state executive power does. ✗\n\nI and II require state ratification. **Answer is (a).**"

                "question": "With reference to the Constitution of India, if an area in a State is declared as Scheduled Area under the Fifth Schedule:\n\nI. The State Government loses its executive power in such areas and a local body assumes total administration.\nII. The Union Government can take over the total administration of such areas under certain circumstances on the recommendations of the Governor.\n\nWhich of the statements given above is/are correct?",
                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "(I) 'State Government loses executive power and local body assumes total administration' — **INCORRECT**. Declaring an area as a Scheduled Area under the Fifth Schedule does NOT strip the State Government of its executive power. The State Government continues to function normally. However, the Governor gets special powers under the Fifth Schedule, including the authority to: direct that particular Acts of Parliament or State Legislature shall not apply to Scheduled Areas, make regulations for peace and good government, and modify existing laws in their application to these areas. Tribes Advisory Councils are constituted, but they ADVISE the Governor — they don't take over 'total administration.' ✗\n\n(II) 'Union Government can take over total administration on Governor's recommendations' — **INCORRECT**. The Fifth Schedule does NOT contain any provision for the Union Government to take over the total administration of Scheduled Areas. The Governor has special regulatory powers, and the President can direct that a particular area be declared/cease to be a Scheduled Area, but there is no mechanism for Union takeover of administration as described. Article 356 (President's Rule) is a separate provision and is not part of the Fifth Schedule framework. ✗\n\nBoth statements are incorrect. **Answer is (d).**",
                "option_a": "I only",
                "option_b": "II only",
                "option_c": "Both I and II",

The Union Government can take over the total administration of such areas under certain circumstances on the recommendations of the Governor.\n\nWhich of the statements given above is/are correct?",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "(I) 'State Government loses executive power and local body assumes total administration' — **INCORRECT**. \n\nDeclaring an area as a Scheduled Area under the Fifth Schedule does NOT strip the State Government of its executive power. \n\nThe State Government continues to function normally. \n\nHowever, the Governor gets special powers under the Fifth Schedule, including the authority to: \n\n- direct that particular Acts of Parliament or State Legislature shall not apply to Scheduled Areas, \n- make regulations for peace and good government, \n- and modify existing laws in their application to these areas. \n\nTribes Advisory Councils are constituted, but they ADVISE the Governor — they don't take over 'total administration.' ✗\n\n(II) 'Union Government can take over total administration on Governor's recommendations' — **INCORRECT**. \n\nThe Fifth Schedule does NOT contain any provision for the Union Government to take over the total administration of Scheduled Areas. \n\nThe Governor has special regulatory powers, and the President can direct that a particular area be declared/cease to be a Scheduled Area, but there is no mechanism for Union takeover of administration as described. \n\nArticle 356 (President's Rule) is a separate provision and is not part of the Fifth Schedule framework. ✗\n\nBoth statements are incorrect. **Answer is (d).**"

                "question": "With reference to the Indian polity, consider the following statements:\n\nI. An Ordinance can amend any Central Act.\nII. An Ordinance can abridge a Fundamental Right.\nIII. An Ordinance can come into effect from a back date.\n\nWhich of the statements given above are correct?",
                "answer": "C",
                "answer_explanation": "Let's evaluate each statement about ordinances:\n\n(I) 'Ordinance can amend any Central Act' — **CORRECT**. An ordinance promulgated under Article 123 has the same force and effect as an Act of Parliament. Therefore, it can modify or amend any existing Central Act. The Supreme Court has affirmed this in multiple cases. ✓\n\n(II) 'Ordinance can abridge a Fundamental Right' — **INCORRECT**. An ordinance, like any other law, is subject to the Fundamental Rights guaranteed in Part III of the Constitution. Article 13(2) states that the State shall not make any law that takes away or abridges Fundamental Rights — and 'law' here includes ordinances. So an ordinance CANNOT abridge Fundamental Rights. If it does, it can be struck down by courts as unconstitutional. ✗\n\n(III) 'Ordinance can come into effect from a back date' — **CORRECT**. An ordinance can have retrospective (back-dated) effect, just like an Act of Parliament can. The government can specify that the ordinance applies from a date prior to its promulgation, within constitutional limits. This has been done in practice, especially for tax-related ordinances. ✓\n\nStatements I and III are correct. **Answer is (c).**",
                "option_a": "I and II only",
                "option_b": "II and III only",
                "option_c": "I and III only",
                "option_d": "I, II and III",
                "pyq_year": "2025",
                "marks": 2,
                "negative_marks": 0.66,
                "formatted_question": "With reference to the Indian polity, consider the following statements:\n\nI. An Ordinance can amend any Central Act.\nII. An Ordinance can abridge a Fundamental Right.\nIII. An Ordinance can come into effect from a back date.\n\nWhich of the statements given above are correct?",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "Let's evaluate each statement about ordinances:\n\n(I) 'Ordinance can amend any Central Act' — **CORRECT**. \n\nAn ordinance promulgated under Article 123 has the same force and effect as an Act of Parliament. \n\nTherefore, it can modify or amend any existing Central Act. \n\nThe Supreme Court has affirmed this in multiple cases. ✓\n\n(II) 'Ordinance can abridge a Fundamental Right' — **INCORRECT**. \n\nAn ordinance, like any other law, is subject to the Fundamental Rights guaranteed in Part III of the Constitution. \n\nArticle 13(2) states that the State shall not make any law that takes away or abridges Fundamental Rights — and 'law' here includes ordinances. \n\nSo an ordinance CANNOT abridge Fundamental Rights. \n\nIf it does, it can be struck down by courts as unconstitutional. ✗\n\n(III) 'Ordinance can come into effect from a back date' — **CORRECT**. \n\nAn ordinance can have retrospective (back-dated) effect, just like an Act of Parliament can. \n\nThe government can specify that the ordinance applies from a date prior to its promulgation, within constitutional limits. \n\nThis has been done in practice, especially for tax-related ordinances. ✓\n\nStatements I and III are correct. **Answer is (c).**"

                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "(I) 'President decides 10th Schedule disqualification based on Council of Ministers' opinion' — **INCORRECT**. Under the Tenth Schedule (anti-defection law), the power to decide on disqualification rests with the presiding officer — the Speaker of the Lok Sabha for its members, and the Chairman of the Rajya Sabha for its members. The President has NO role in deciding anti-defection cases. The Supreme Court, in Kihoto Hollohan v. Zachillhu (1992), upheld the Speaker's authority while making the Speaker's decision subject to judicial review. ✗\n\n(II) 'No mention of political party in the Constitution' — **INCORRECT**. The Constitution of India does mention 'political party.' The term appears explicitly in the Tenth Schedule (added by the 52nd Amendment Act, 1985), which deals with defection. Paragraph 2 of the Tenth Schedule specifically uses the term 'political party' when describing grounds for disqualification. Article 102(2) and Article 191(2) also reference the Tenth Schedule. ✗\n\nBoth statements are incorrect. **Answer is (d).**",

                "question": "Consider the following statements:\n\nI. On the dissolution of the House of the People, the Speaker shall not vacate his/her office until immediately before the first meeting of the House of the People after the dissolution.\nII. According to the provisions of the Constitution of India, a Member of the House of the People on being elected as Speaker shall resign from his/her political party immediately.\nIII. The Speaker of the House of the People may be removed from his/her office by a resolution of the House of the People passed by a majority of all the then Members of the House, provided that no resolution shall be moved unless at least fourteen days' notice has been given of the intention to move the resolution.\n\nWhich of the statements given above are correct?",
                "answer": "C",
                "answer_explanation": "(I) 'Speaker doesn't vacate office on dissolution until before the first meeting of new House' — **CORRECT**. Article 94 provides that the Speaker continues in office even after the Lok Sabha is dissolved, until just before the first meeting of the newly elected House. This ensures continuity of the office and allows the outgoing Speaker to oversee procedural matters during the transition period. ✓\n\n(II) 'Constitution requires Speaker to resign from political party' — **INCORRECT**. There is no such constitutional provision. The Constitution does NOT require the Speaker to resign from their political party. In practice, Speakers are expected to be impartial, and some Speakers have voluntarily resigned from their party (like N. Sanjiva Reddy), but this is a convention, not a constitutional requirement. In the UK system, the Speaker does resign from their party, but India has not adopted this practice constitutionally. ✗\n\n(III) 'Speaker can be removed by majority of all then Members with 14 days' notice' — **CORRECT**. Under Article 94, the Speaker can be removed by a resolution passed by a majority of ALL the then members of the House (effective majority, not just those present and voting). Importantly, at least 14 days' advance notice must be given before moving such a resolution. While the removal resolution is being considered, the Speaker cannot preside over the House. ✓\n\nStatements I and III are correct. **Answer is (c).**",
                "option_a": "I and II only",
                "option_b": "II and III only",

You don't have the authority to hear this case.' \n\nKey points: \n- It can only be issued against **judicial** and **quasi-judicial bodies** (courts, tribunals). \n- It **CANNOT** be issued against **administrative authorities**, **legislatures**, or **private persons**. \n- It is the opposite of **Mandamus** — Prohibition says 'don't do it,' while Mandamus says 'do it.'"

                "question": "What is the position of the Right to Property in India?",
                "answer": "B",
                "answer_explanation": "Article 300A states: 'No person shall be deprived of his property save by authority of law.' The key word is 'person' — not 'citizen.' This means Right to Property is available to all persons in India, including foreigners. It was originally a Fundamental Right under Article 31, but the 44th Amendment (1978) removed it from Fundamental Rights, making it a constitutional/legal right under Article 300A. Since it says 'person' not 'citizen,' it is a legal right available to any person.\n\nAnswer: (b).",
                "option_a": "Legal right available to citizens only",
                "option_b": "Legal right available to any person",
                "option_c": "Fundamental Right available to citizens only",
                "option_d": "Neither Fundamental Right nor legal right",

The question asks which one.\n\nA (Creating new All India Services) — Not the answer. Under Article 312, Rajya Sabha can pass a resolution by 2/3 majority to create new All India Services. This is actually a SPECIAL power of Rajya Sabha alone.\n\nB (Amending the Constitution) — **CORRECT**: For Constitutional Amendment Bills, both Houses have equal power. The bill must be passed by both Lok Sabha AND Rajya Sabha with a special majority (2/3 of members present and voting, and more than 50% of total membership). Neither House is superior here.\n\nC (Removal of government) — Incorrect. Only Lok Sabha can remove the government through a no-confidence motion. Rajya Sabha has NO power to remove the government.\n\nD (Making cut motions) — Incorrect. Cut motions are related to budget/money bills, which are the exclusive domain of Lok Sabha. Rajya Sabha can only recommend changes but cannot reject money bills.\n\n**Answer: B.**\n\n**Key Takeaway:** Rajya Sabha = Lok Sabha in: Constitutional amendments, election/impeachment of President, election of VP. The question asks which one.\n\nA (Creating new All India Services) — Not the answer. Under Article 312, Rajya Sabha can pass a resolution by *2/3 majority* to create new All India Services. This is actually a *SPECIAL* power of Rajya Sabha alone.\n\nB (Amending the Constitution) — **CORRECT**: For Constitutional Amendment Bills, both Houses have *equal power*. The bill must be passed by both Lok Sabha *AND* Rajya Sabha with a *special majority* (*2/3 of members present and voting*, and *more than 50% of total membership*). Neither House is superior here.\n\nC (Removal of government) — Incorrect. Only Lok Sabha can remove the government through a *no-confidence motion*. Rajya Sabha has *NO* power to remove the government.\n\nD (Making cut motions) — Incorrect. Cut motions are related to *budget/money bills*, which are the exclusive domain of Lok Sabha.                 "question": "The Preamble to the Constitution of India, is",
                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "The Preamble's legal status has been debated through several Supreme Court cases:\n\n1. Berubari case (1960): SC said the Preamble is NOT part of the Constitution.\n2. Kesavananda Bharati case (1973): SC overruled Berubari and said the Preamble IS part of the Constitution and its objectives form part of the Basic Structure.\n3. LIC of India case (1995): SC confirmed the Preamble is an integral part of the Constitution.\n\nHowever, the Preamble has NO independent legal effect. This means you cannot go to court and say \"this law violates the Preamble\" on its own. The Preamble only works through other parts of the Constitution. For example, the word \"secular\" in the Preamble is enforced through the Fundamental Right to Freedom of Religion (Articles 25-28). If a law violates secularism, it's struck down for violating Fundamental Rights, not the Preamble directly.\n\nSo the Preamble IS part of the Constitution, but it has no legal power independent of other provisions.\n\n**Answer: D.**\n\n**Key Takeaway:** Preamble = part of Constitution (after Kesavananda case) + no independent legal effect. It's a guiding light, not a standalone legal weapon.",
                "option_a": "part of the Constitution but has no legal effect",
                "option_b": "not a part of the Constitution and has no legal effect either",
                "option_c": "a part of the Constitution and has the same legal effect as any other part",
                "option_d": "a part of the Constitution but has no legal effect independently of other parts",
                "pyq_year": "2020",
                "marks": 4,
                "negative_marks": 1,
                "formatted_question": "The Preamble to the Constitution of India, is",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "The Preamble's legal status has been debated through several Supreme Court cases:\n\n1. **Berubari case (1960)**: SC said the Preamble is **NOT** part of the Constitution.\n\n2. **Kesavananda Bharati case (1973)**: SC overruled Berubari and said the Preamble **IS** part of the Constitution and its objectives form part of the **Basic Structure**.\n\n3. **LIC of India case (1995)**: SC confirmed the Preamble is an **integral part** of the Constitution.\n\nHowever, the Preamble has **NO** independent legal effect. \n\nThis means you cannot go to court and say \"this law violates the Preamble\" on its own. \n\nThe Preamble only works through other parts of the Constitution. \n\nFor example, the word \"secular\" in the Preamble is enforced through the **Fundamental Right to Freedom of Religion** (Articles 25-28). \n\nIf a law violates secularism, it's struck down for violating **Fundamental Rights**, not the Preamble directly.\n\nSo the Preamble **IS** part of the Constitution, but it has no legal power independent of other provisions. \n\n**Answer: D.**\n\n**Key Takeaway:** Preamble = part of Constitution (after Kesavananda case) + no independent legal effect. It's a guiding light, not a standalone legal weapon."

                "answer": "B",
                "answer_explanation": "**Statement 1** (Constitution defines its basic structure) — **NOT CORRECT**: The Constitution does NOT explicitly define its \"basic structure\" anywhere in its text. The \"basic structure doctrine\" was created by the Supreme Court in the Kesavananda Bharati case (1973). The Court said Parliament cannot amend the Constitution in a way that destroys its basic structure — but what constitutes \"basic structure\" has been identified through various court judgments over time (federalism, secularism, democracy, judicial review, etc.). The Constitution itself doesn't list these.\n\n**Statement 2** (Constitution provides for judicial review) — **CORRECT**: While the exact term \"judicial review\" is not written in the Constitution, the power is implied through several articles. Article 13 says laws violating fundamental rights are void. Articles 32 and 226 allow the Supreme Court and High Courts to issue writs to protect rights. Together, these provisions give courts the power to review and strike down laws — which IS judicial review.\n\n**Answer: B (2 only).**\n\n**Key Takeaway:** \"Basic structure\" was defined by the Supreme Court, NOT written in the Constitution.                 "formatted_answer_explanation": "**Statement 1** (Constitution defines its basic structure) — **NOT CORRECT**: \n\nThe Constitution does NOT explicitly define its \"basic structure\" anywhere in its text. \n\nThe \"basic structure doctrine\" was created by the Supreme Court in the **Kesavananda Bharati** case (**1973**). \n\nThe Court said Parliament cannot amend the Constitution in a way that destroys its basic structure — but what constitutes \"basic structure\" has been identified through various court judgments over time (**federalism, secularism, democracy, judicial review, etc.**). \n\nThe Constitution itself doesn't list these.\n\n**Statement 2** (Constitution provides for judicial review) — **CORRECT**: \n\nWhile the exact term \"judicial review\" is not written in the Constitution, the power is implied through several articles. \n\n- **Article 13** says laws violating fundamental rights are void. \n- **Articles 32** and **226** allow the Supreme Court and High Courts to issue writs to protect rights. \n\nTogether, these provisions give courts the power to review and strike down laws — which IS judicial review.\n\n**Answer: B (2 only).**\n\n**Key Takeaway:** \"Basic structure\" was defined by the Supreme Court, NOT written in the Constitution. \n\nJudicial review is implied (not explicitly mentioned) but is a reality through **Articles 13, 32, and 226**."

                "answer": "C",

Limitation of Powers — CORRECT: Rule of Law means that even the government's power is limited by law. No one, including the government, is above the law. Fundamental Rights in Part III of the Constitution limit Parliament's law-making power.\n\n2. Equality before law — CORRECT: Everyone is equal before the law. No person gets special treatment based on their position, wealth, or status. This is enshrined in Article 14 of the Indian Constitution.\n\n3. People's responsibility to the Government — WRONG: Rule of Law is about the GOVERNMENT's accountability to the PEOPLE, not the other way around. It protects individuals from arbitrary government action.\n\n4. Liberty and civil rights — CORRECT: Rule of Law ensures that individuals enjoy liberty and civil rights. The government cannot deprive anyone of life or liberty without following proper legal procedures (Article 21).\n\n> **REMEMBER:** Rule of Law = (1) Government's power is limited, (2) Everyone is equal before law, (3) Liberty is protected. Puttaswamy vs Union of India (2017)**. The Court ruled that:\n\n- **Right to Privacy** is an intrinsic (built-in) part of **Right to Life** and **Personal Liberty** under **Article 21**.\n- It is also connected to the freedoms guaranteed under **Part III** (**Fundamental Rights**) of the Constitution.\n\n**Why the other options are wrong:**\n\n- **Option A** (**Article 14 + 42nd Amendment**): Article 14 is about equality, and the 42nd Amendment is not specifically about privacy.\n- **Option B** (**Article 17 + DPSPs**): Article 17 abolishes untouchability — unrelated to privacy.\n- **Option D** (**Article 24 + 44th Amendment**): Article 24 prohibits child labour in factories — unrelated to privacy.\n\nThe key point: The **9-judge bench** unanimously declared that privacy is a fundamental right protected under **Article 21**. This overruled earlier judgments (**M.P. Sharma 1954** and **Kharak Singh 1962**) that had held otherwise.\n\n**REMEMBER:** Right to Privacy = **Article 21** + **Part III**. **Puttaswamy case (2017)** = **9-judge bench** = unanimous decision.                 "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "The Preamble is considered the 'key to the minds of the makers' of the Constitution. This phrase comes from the Supreme Court's landmark judgment in the Berubari Union case (1960), where the Court observed that the Preamble shows the general purposes behind the several provisions in the Constitution and is thus a key to the minds of the Constitution-makers. The Preamble encapsulates the fundamental values and philosophy that guided the framers — Justice (social, economic, political), Liberty (of thought, expression, belief, faith, worship), Equality (of status and opportunity), and Fraternity (assuring dignity of the individual and unity of the nation). While Fundamental Rights (option b), Directive Principles (option c), and Fundamental Duties (option d) are all important parts of the Constitution, they deal with specific aspects. The Preamble, on the other hand, provides the overarching vision and summary of what the entire Constitution seeks to achieve. Think of it as the 'introduction' or 'mission statement' of the Constitution that reflects the collective aspirations and philosophy of those who drafted it.\n\n*(Source: Laxmikant, Chapter on Preamble)*",

                "option_b": "The Fundamental Rights",

This means the Council of Ministers (Cabinet) as a whole is accountable to the Lok Sabha (or State Assembly). If the legislature loses confidence in the Cabinet, the entire ministry must resign. This is the defining feature that distinguishes parliamentary government from presidential government.\n\n**Option (a)** is wrong —** the purpose of Cabinet government is not to minimize criticism.\n\n**Option (b)** is wrong —** while Cabinet government can be efficient, its core principle is accountability, not speed.\n\n**Option (d)** is wrong —** Cabinet government is not a device for strengthening a declining leader's hold. The key features of parliamentary government in India include: (a) Presence of nominal (President) and real (Prime Minister) executives, (b) Majority party rule, (c) Collective responsibility of the executive to the legislature, (d) Ministers must be members of the legislature, (e) Leadership of the Prime Minister, and (f) Power to dissolve the lower house. Among all these, collective responsibility is the central organizing principle.\n\n*(Source: Laxmikant, Chapter on Salient Features of the Constitution)*",

Note the careful wording — 'not necessarily' means it may or may not happen.\n\n**Item 1 —** Dissolution of the State Legislative Assembly: This is NOT a necessary consequence. When President's Rule is imposed under Article 356, the President may either DISSOLVE or merely SUSPEND the State Legislative Assembly. Suspension means the assembly is kept in abeyance and can be revived later; dissolution means it ceases to exist entirely and fresh elections must be held. Since dissolution is optional (suspension is also possible), item 1 is NOT a necessary consequence.\n\n**Item 2 —** Removal of the Council of Ministers: This IS a necessary consequence. When President's Rule is imposed, the state Council of Ministers headed by the Chief Minister is ALWAYS dismissed. The Governor then administers the state on behalf of the President with the help of advisors. This is always done — it is the very essence of President's Rule.\n\n**Item 3 —** Dissolution of local bodies: This is NOT a consequence of President's Rule at all. Local bodies (Panchayats, Municipalities) function under separate constitutional provisions (73rd and 74th Amendments) and are not dissolved simply because President's Rule is imposed in the state.\n\nSo items 1 and 3 are NOT necessarily consequences, while item 2 IS a necessary consequence. The answer is (b) 1 and 3 only.\n\n*(Source: Laxmikant, Chapter on Emergency Provisions)*",
                "option_a": "1 and 2 only",
                "option_b": "1 and 3 only",

                "answer_explanation": "**Statement 1 is incorrect:** India follows the First-Past-The-Post (FPTP) system for Lok Sabha and State Assembly elections, NOT a majority system requiring 50% of votes. Under FPTP, the candidate who gets more votes than any other candidate wins — even if that number is far less than 50%. For example, in a constituency with 5 candidates, someone could win with just 25-30% of votes as long as they got more than everyone else. The 50% requirement exists in some other countries (like France's two-round system), but NOT in India.\n\n**Statement 2 is incorrect:** The Constitution does NOT specify that the Speaker's post must go to the majority party and the Deputy Speaker's post to the Opposition. Article 93 (for Lok Sabha) simply says that the House shall choose two of its members to be Speaker and Deputy Speaker. There is no constitutional provision mandating party-based allocation. In practice, while the Speaker usually comes from the ruling party and the Deputy Speaker is sometimes offered to the Opposition by convention, this is a political practice, not a constitutional requirement.\n\nSince both statements are wrong, the answer is (d).\n\n*(Source: Laxmikant, Chapter on Parliament)*",

                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "**All three are devices through which Parliament exercises control over the Council of Ministers: (1) Adjournment Motion: This is a motion moved by a member to draw the attention of the House to a matter of urgent public importance.** If admitted, it leads to a debate and the regular business of the House is set aside. It serves as a censure (criticism) of the government and allows Parliament to hold ministers accountable on urgent matters. It is available only in Lok Sabha.\n\n(2) Question Hour: The first hour of every parliamentary sitting (11 AM to 12 noon) is devoted to questions. Members ask questions to ministers about the functioning of their ministries and departments. This is one of the most effective tools of parliamentary oversight — it forces ministers to be prepared and accountable for their department's actions. Questions can be starred (requiring oral answer with supplementary questions), unstarred (written answer), or short notice questions.\n\n(3) Supplementary Questions: During Question Hour, after a minister answers a starred question, other members can ask follow-up (supplementary) questions on the same topic. These are spontaneous and cannot be prepared in advance by ministers, making them a powerful tool for extracting information and holding the government accountable. Other devices for parliamentary control include no-confidence motion, calling attention motion, half-an-hour discussion, and zero hour. All three listed devices are valid, so the answer is (d).\n\n*(Source: Laxmikant, Chapter on Parliamentary System)*",

                "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "In political theory, rights and duties are correlative — meaning they are interconnected and mutually dependent. A right of one person implies a corresponding duty on others to respect that right. For example, your right to life implies a duty on others not to harm you; your right to free speech implies a duty on the state not to suppress your expression. Without duties, rights would be meaningless, and without rights, duties would have no purpose.\n\n**Option (b)** is wrong because rights are NOT purely personal and independent of society**. Rights exist within a social context — they define the relationship between individuals and between individuals and the state. Robinson Crusoe on a deserted island has no 'rights' because there is no society to recognize them.\n\n**Option (c)** is wrong because it falsely suggests duties are unimportant for personality development**. Both rights AND duties contribute to a citizen's growth.\n\n**Option (d)** is wrong because it falsely suggests rights are unimportant for state stability**. Both are essential. The correct understanding is that rights and duties are two sides of the same coin — they coexist and reinforce each other.\n\n*(Source: NIOS, Democracy at Work, Chapter on Fundamental Rights and Duties)*",

If a law or executive order is found to violate the Constitution (i.e., is ultra vires or beyond the power granted), the court can declare it illegal, unconstitutional, and void — meaning the government cannot enforce it. The constitutional validity of a law can be challenged on three grounds: (a) it infringes Fundamental Rights (Part III), (b) it is outside the competence of the authority that made it (e.g., a state legislature making a law on a Union List subject), or (c) it is repugnant to constitutional provisions.\n\n**Option (b)** is wrong:** Judicial review is NOT about questioning the 'wisdom' or 'merit' of a law. Courts do not judge whether a law is good policy or bad policy — they only check whether it is constitutionally valid. This is an important distinction.\n\n**Option (c)** is wrong:** Courts do NOT review all legislative enactments BEFORE they receive Presidential assent. Judicial review happens AFTER a law is enacted, usually when someone challenges it in court. India does not have a system of pre-legislative judicial review.\n\n**Option (d)** is wrong:** Reviewing earlier judgements is called 'review jurisdiction' or 'curative petition', which is different from 'judicial review'.\n\n*(Source: Laxmikant, Chapter on High Court and Supreme Court)*",

                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "**Both statements are incorrect.**\n\n**Statement 1 is incorrect:** The Constitution does NOT provide any legislative process or enforcement mechanism for Fundamental Duties. Unlike Fundamental Rights (which are justiciable and enforceable through courts under Articles 32 and 226), Fundamental Duties (Article 51A, added by the 42nd Amendment in 1976) are non-justiciable — meaning no one can approach a court to enforce them, and there is no legal sanction or penalty prescribed in the Constitution for their violation. Parliament can enact laws to enforce them, but the Constitution itself does not provide such a process.\n\n**Statement 2 is incorrect:** Fundamental Duties are NOT correlative to legal duties. 'Correlative' means corresponding or complementary. Legal duties are binding obligations enforceable by law (like paying taxes, obeying traffic rules). Fundamental Duties are moral obligations and civic ideals (like respecting the national flag, protecting the environment, developing scientific temper) — but they do not create legally enforceable obligations in the same way. They serve as reminders to citizens about their responsibilities but lack the legal teeth of actual legal duties. The Swaran Singh Committee (1976) recommended their inclusion, but also suggested penalties for non-compliance — this recommendation was NOT accepted, reinforcing their non-enforceable nature.\n\nSo neither statement is true.\n\n*(Source: Laxmikant, Chapter on Fundamental Duties)*",

                "question": "Consider the following statements: With reference to the Constitution of India, the Directive Principles of State Policy constitute limitations upon\n\n1. legislative function.\n2. executive function.\n\nWhich of the above statements is/are correct?",
                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "**Neither statement is correct.** The Directive Principles of State Policy (Part IV, Articles 36-51) are described in Article 37 as 'fundamental in the governance of the country' and it is 'the duty of the State to apply these principles in making laws'. However, they are expressly made NON-JUSTICIABLE — meaning they cannot be enforced by any court. This means DPSPs do NOT constitute 'limitations' on either the legislative or executive function in the legal sense. Limitations on government power come from Fundamental Rights (Part III), which ARE justiciable and enforceable. If Parliament passes a law that violates Fundamental Rights, courts can strike it down. But if Parliament fails to implement a Directive Principle, no court can compel it to do so. DPSPs serve as guidelines and aspirational goals for the State, not as legal constraints. They tell the government what it SHOULD do, but they do not legally PREVENT it from doing (or not doing) anything. The question's framing — 'constitute limitations upon' — implies legal restrictions, which DPSPs are not. Some students confuse DPSPs with Fundamental Rights because both are in Part III and IV of the Constitution. The key distinction: Fundamental Rights = justiciable = limitations on State power. Directive Principles = non-justiciable = guidelines for State action, NOT limitations.\n\n*(Source: Laxmikant, Chapter on Directive Principles of State Policy)*",

The Representation of the People Act, 1951 governs elections in India, and the right to vote flows from this statute, not directly from the Fundamental Rights chapter of the Constitution.\n\n**Option (a)** is wrong because the right to vote is NOT listed under Part III (Fundamental Rights) of the Constitution**.                 "question": "Local self-government can be best explained as an exercise in",
                "answer": "B",
                "answer_explanation": "Local self-government is best described as an exercise in democratic decentralization. Democratic decentralization means transferring power, authority, and resources from the central/state government to local elected bodies (like Panchayats and Municipalities) so that people at the grassroots level can participate in governance and decision-making that directly affects their lives. The concept gained prominence in India through the Balwant Rai Mehta Committee (1957), which recommended establishing a scheme of 'democratic decentralisation' — later known as Panchayati Raj. The 73rd and 74th Constitutional Amendments (1992) gave constitutional status to Panchayats and Municipalities respectively.\n\n**Option (a)** 'Federalism' is wrong — federalism refers to the division of powers between the Centre and States, not the devolution of power to local bodies.\n\n**Option (c)** 'Administrative delegation' is wrong — this implies merely assigning administrative tasks to lower officials without giving them independent decision-making authority or electoral legitimacy. Local self-government goes beyond mere delegation — it involves elected bodies with their own powers.\n\n**Option (d)** 'Direct democracy' is wrong — direct democracy means citizens directly vote on every issue (like in ancient Athens or Swiss referendums). Indian local self-government operates through elected representatives, making it representative democracy at the local level, not direct democracy.\n\n*(Source: Laxmikant, Chapter on Panchayati Raj)*",

                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "**Both statements are incorrect.**\n\n**Statement 1 is incorrect:** A Private Member's Bill is a bill introduced by any Member of Parliament who is NOT a minister. It has nothing to do with whether the member is elected or nominated. Both elected and nominated members can introduce private member's bills — the only criterion is that the member should not be a minister. The distinction is between 'Government Bills' (introduced by ministers) and 'Private Member's Bills' (introduced by non-minister MPs). A private member's bill requires one month's notice for introduction, and its drafting is the responsibility of the member concerned (unlike government bills, which are drafted by the concerned ministry).\n\n**Statement 2 is incorrect:** Private member's bills have been passed by Parliament before — this is not a first-time event. The last Private Member's Bill passed by Parliament was the Supreme Court (Enlargement of Criminal Appellate Jurisdiction) Bill, 1968, which became an Act in 1970. What happened recently (in 2015) was that the Rights of Transgender Persons Bill, 2014 was passed by the Rajya Sabha — this was notable because it was the first private member's bill to get the upper house's approval in 45 years, but it was NOT the first time ever in India's parliamentary history. Originally there were 10 duties; an 11th was added by the 86th Amendment (2002).                 "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "As per Laxmikant (4th Edition, pg 149): The defining feature of a Parliamentary System (as opposed to a Presidential System) is that the executive is responsible to the legislature. In India, this is expressed through Article 75: 'The Council of Ministers shall be collectively responsible to the House of the People (Lok Sabha).' This means.\n\n(1) The Council of Ministers stays in power only as long as it enjoys the confidence (majority support) of the Lok Sabha.\n\n(2) If the Lok Sabha passes a no-confidence motion against the Council of Ministers, the entire ministry must resign.\n\n(3) Individual ministers are also responsible — they must defend their actions before Parliament. This principle of COLLECTIVE RESPONSIBILITY to the lower house is the bedrock of the parliamentary system. It is what makes India parliamentary and not presidential. Why are the other options not the defining reason?\n\n(a) 'Lok Sabha is elected directly by the people' — direct election of the lower house exists in presidential systems too (e.g., the US House of Representatives). So this alone does not make a system parliamentary.\n\n(b) 'Parliament can amend the Constitution' — many countries with different systems can amend their constitutions. This is not unique to parliamentary systems.\n\n(c) 'Rajya Sabha cannot be dissolved' — this is a feature of the Indian system, but it does not define the parliamentary nature. Even in the UK (the original parliamentary model), the House of Lords is permanent.\n\n**Answer: (d)**.\n\n**Key distinction:** Parliamentary system = executive responsible to legislature. Presidential system = executive independent of legislature.",
                "option_a": "Lok Sabha is elected directly by the people",
                "option_b": "Parliament can amend the Constitution",
                "option_c": "Rajya Sabha cannot be dissolved",
                "option_d": "Council of Ministers is responsible to the Lok Sabha",
                "pyq_year": "2015",
                "marks": 2,
                "negative_marks": 0.66,
                "formatted_question": "There is a Parliamentary System of Government in India because the",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "As per Laxmikant (4th Edition, pg 149): \n\nThe defining feature of a *Parliamentary System* (as opposed to a *Presidential System*) is that the *executive is responsible to the legislature*. \n\nIn India, this is expressed through *Article 75*: \n\n'The Council of Ministers shall be collectively responsible to the House of the People (Lok Sabha).' \n\nThis means:\n\n(1) The *Council of Ministers* stays in power only as long as it enjoys the *confidence* (majority support) of the *Lok Sabha*.\n\n(2) If the *Lok Sabha* passes a *no-confidence motion* against the *Council of Ministers*, the entire ministry must resign.\n\n(3) Individual ministers are also responsible — they must defend their actions before *Parliament*. \n\nThis principle of *COLLECTIVE RESPONSIBILITY* to the lower house is the bedrock of the parliamentary system. \n\nIt is what makes India parliamentary and not presidential. \n\nWhy are the other options not the defining reason?\n\n(a) 'Lok Sabha is elected directly by the people' — direct election of the lower house exists in presidential systems too (e.g., the *US House of Representatives*). So this alone does not make a system parliamentary.\n\n(b) 'Parliament can amend the Constitution' — many countries with different systems can amend their constitutions. This is not unique to parliamentary systems.\n\n(c) 'Rajya Sabha cannot be dissolved' — this is a feature of the Indian system, but it does not define the parliamentary nature. Even in the *UK* (the original parliamentary model), the *House of Lords* is permanent.\n\n**Answer: (d)**.\n\n**Key distinction:** \n\n*Parliamentary system* = executive responsible to legislature. \n\n*Presidential system* = executive independent of legislature."

                "question": "When a bill is referred to a joint sitting of both the Houses of the Parliament, it has to be passed by",
                "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "As per Laxmikant (pg 22.3-22.4): A joint sitting of both Houses of Parliament is an extraordinary mechanism provided under Article 108 of the Constitution to resolve a deadlock between Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha on an ordinary bill. The key rule is simple: at a joint sitting, the bill is passed by a SIMPLE MAJORITY of members of both Houses present and voting. There is no requirement for any special majority. This actually works in favour of Lok Sabha because it has roughly double the members compared to Rajya Sabha (545 vs 245), so Lok Sabha's numbers naturally dominate in a joint sitting.\n\n**Important distinctions to remember:** Joint sitting of Parliament = Simple majority of members present and voting. Constitutional Amendment Bill (Article 368) = Special majority (majority of total membership + two-thirds of members present and voting) in EACH House separately — no provision for joint sitting. Impeachment of President = Special majority in each House separately.\n\nThe other options — three-fourths majority, two-thirds majority, and absolute majority — are requirements for other specific procedures but NOT for a joint sitting. Only 3 joint sittings have been held so far in Indian parliamentary history (Dowry Prohibition Bill 1961, Banking Service Commission Bill 1978, POTA 2002).",

                "formatted_answer_explanation": "As per **Laxmikant** (pg 22.3-22.4): \n\nA joint sitting of both Houses of Parliament is an extraordinary mechanism provided under **Article 108** of the Constitution to resolve a deadlock between **Lok Sabha** and **Rajya Sabha** on an ordinary bill. \n\nThe key rule is simple: at a joint sitting, the bill is passed by a **SIMPLE MAJORITY** of members of both Houses present and voting. \n\nThere is no requirement for any special majority. \n\nThis actually works in favour of **Lok Sabha** because it has roughly double the members compared to **Rajya Sabha** (545 vs 245), so **Lok Sabha's** numbers naturally dominate in a joint sitting.\n\n**Important distinctions to remember:**\n\n- Joint sitting of Parliament = Simple majority of members present and voting. \n- Constitutional Amendment Bill (**Article 368**) = Special majority (majority of total membership + two-thirds of members present and voting) in EACH House separately — no provision for joint sitting. \n- Impeachment of President = Special majority in each House separately.\n\nThe other options — **three-fourths majority**, **two-thirds majority**, and **absolute majority** — are requirements for other specific procedures but NOT for a joint sitting. \n\nOnly **3 joint sittings** have been held so far in Indian parliamentary history (**Dowry Prohibition Bill 1961**, **Banking Service Commission Bill 1978**, **POTA 2002**)."

                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "As per CBSE Legal Studies textbook (Unit 4, Chapter 1, pg 165): The Supreme Court of India is considered the custodian and guardian of the Constitution. In a parliamentary democracy like India, the Constitution is the supreme law, and someone must ensure that no one — not Parliament, not the executive, not the states — violates it. That role belongs to the Supreme Court through its power of JUDICIAL REVIEW (the authority to examine whether any law or executive action is consistent with the Constitution and to strike it down if it is not). The Supreme Court protects the Constitution by.\n\n(1) interpreting constitutional provisions and settling disputes about their meaning,\n\n(2) striking down laws that violate fundamental rights or exceed legislative competence (Article 13),\n\n(3) deciding disputes between the Union and States or between States (Article 131), and (4) protecting citizens' fundamental rights through writs under Article 32.\n\n**Why are the other options wrong?**\n\n(a) The President is the HEAD OF STATE and takes an oath to 'preserve, protect and defend' the Constitution, but the President acts on the advice of the Council of Ministers (Article 74) and cannot independently review laws for constitutionality.\n\n(b) The Prime Minister heads the government but does not perform the judicial review function.\n\n(c) The Lok Sabha Secretariat is an administrative body that assists Parliament — it has no constitutional guardianship role.\n\n**Key concept:** Custodian of Constitution = Supreme Court (through judicial review). Guardian of public purse = Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG).",

                "formatted_answer_explanation": "As per **CBSE Legal Studies textbook** (Unit 4, Chapter 1, pg 165): \n\nThe **Supreme Court of India** is considered the *custodian* and *guardian* of the **Constitution**. \n\nIn a parliamentary democracy like India, the Constitution is the *supreme law*, and someone must ensure that no one — not Parliament, not the executive, not the states — violates it. \n\nThat role belongs to the Supreme Court through its power of **JUDICIAL REVIEW** (the authority to examine whether any law or executive action is consistent with the Constitution and to strike it down if it is not). \n\nThe Supreme Court protects the Constitution by:\n\n(1) interpreting constitutional provisions and settling disputes about their meaning,\n\n(2) striking down laws that violate fundamental rights or exceed legislative competence (**Article 13**),\n\n(3) deciding disputes between the Union and States or between States (**Article 131**), and \n\n(4) protecting citizens' fundamental rights through writs under **Article 32**.\n\n**Why are the other options wrong?**\n\n(a) The **President** is the *HEAD OF STATE* and takes an oath to 'preserve, protect and defend' the Constitution, but the President acts on the advice of the **Council of Ministers** (**Article 74**) and cannot independently review laws for constitutionality.\n\n(b) The **Prime Minister** heads the government but does not perform the judicial review function.\n\n(c) The **Lok Sabha Secretariat** is an administrative body that assists Parliament — it has no constitutional guardianship role.\n\n**Key concept:** \n\n*Custodian of Constitution* = **Supreme Court** (through judicial review). \n\n*Guardian of public purse* = **Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG)**."

The Governor can recommend President's Rule if they believe the state government cannot function per the Constitution. No ministerial advice is needed for this.\n\n(2) Appointing Ministers — WRONG: The Governor appoints the CM, and then other Ministers are appointed ON THE ADVICE of the CM (Article 164). This is NOT discretionary — the Governor is bound by the CM's recommendations. Exception: The choice of CM itself can involve discretion when no party has a clear majority.\n\n(3) Reserving bills for President's consideration (Article 200) — CORRECT: The Governor can use personal judgment to reserve certain State bills for the President.                 "formatted_answer_explanation": "**Article 124**\n\n(1) says the **Supreme Court** shall consist of a **Chief Justice** and such number of other judges as **Parliament** may by law prescribe. \n\nSo it is **PARLIAMENT** that decides the strength of the **SC** through legislation (like the **Supreme Court (Number of Judges) Act**). \n\nThe **President** appoints judges but cannot increase the number. \n\nThe **CJI** recommends names for appointment but doesn't decide strength. \n\nThe **Law Commission** advises on legal reforms but has no such power. \n\n**Current strength:** Originally **8 judges** (1950), increased multiple times by **Parliament** — now **34** (including **CJI**).\n\n(Ref: **Laxmikanth**)"

                "answer": "B",
                "answer_explanation": "This is a conceptual question about constitutionalism. The core idea of a Constitutional Government is LIMITED GOVERNMENT — the Constitution exists primarily to RESTRICT STATE POWER so that individual liberty is protected.\n\n**Statement 1** — WRONG: This describes an authoritarian/totalitarian government where individual freedom is curtailed for state power. This is the OPPOSITE of constitutionalism.\n\n**Statement 2** — CORRECT: A Constitutional Government places checks on state authority (through separation of powers, fundamental rights, judicial review, rule of law) to protect individual liberty. Think of it this way: The Constitution is a contract between the people and the state, where the people agree to give the state some power, BUT the Constitution limits how much power the state can exercise. Without these limits, you get dictatorship.\n\n(Ref: Arora and Goyal, Chapter on Constitutionalism)",

                "formatted_answer_explanation": "Under the **Forest Rights Act (FRA), 2006**, the process of determining forest rights begins at the **grassroots level**. \n\n**Chapter IV, Clause 6(1)** of the Act clearly states that the **Gram Sabha** shall be the authority to initiate the process for determining the nature and extent of individual or community forest rights.\n\nThis is a **bottom-up approach** — the process starts with the community itself, not with government officials. \n\nThe **Gram Sabha** receives claims, verifies them through a **Forest Rights Committee (FRC)**, prepares a map of the forest area, and then passes a resolution. \n\nThe claims then go upward to the **Sub-Divisional Level Committee** and **District Level Committee** for approval.\n\nThe **State Forest Department** (a), **District Collector** (b), and **Tahsildar/BDO** (c) are **NOT** the initiating authorities — the **Gram Sabha** is.\n\nThis provision was prominently tested in the **Vedanta Mining case** in **Odisha (Niyamgiri Hills)**, where the **Supreme Court** upheld the role of **Gram Sabhas**."

                "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "A joint sitting of Parliament (Article 108) is called ONLY for ordinary legislation when there is a deadlock between the two Houses.\nIt does NOT apply to: Money Bills (statement 2) — because Rajya Sabha can only delay a Money Bill by 14 days, and Lok Sabha's will prevails automatically. No deadlock is possible.\nConstitutional Amendment Bills (statement 3) — because Article 368 requires each House to pass the bill separately by special majority.                 "formatted_answer_explanation": "An **adjournment motion** is a parliamentary device to draw urgent attention to a recent, definite matter of public importance. \n\nIt interrupts normal business of the House. \n\nDon't confuse it with: \n\n- (b) **Question Hour** — which is for collecting information from ministers; \n- (c) **Cut Motion** — which is used to reduce a specific amount in a demand for grant; \n- (d) **Adjournment of the House** — the Speaker can adjourn proceedings for disorder, but that is not an 'adjournment motion'. \n\nSource: **Laxmikant**."

                "answer": "C",
                "answer_explanation": "The answer is (c) — Consolidated Fund of India (Article 266). India has THREE types of government funds:\n\n(1) CONSOLIDATED FUND OF INDIA: ALL revenues (taxes, duties, fees) + all loans raised + all money received in repayment of loans go HERE. This is the main government account. No money can be spent from it without Parliament's approval.\n\n(2) PUBLIC ACCOUNT: Money held by government in TRUST — like provident funds, small savings, judicial deposits. Government acts as a banker, not owner. Parliament approval NOT needed for withdrawals.\n\n(3) CONTINGENCY FUND: A small fund (₹500 crore) at the President's disposal for EMERGENCY unforeseen expenses. Like a 'petty cash' fund. Parliament must later approve the spending.\n\n**Simple analogy:** Consolidated Fund = your salary account (main income). Public Account = money you hold for others (security deposits).
