            "single": {
                "question": "When the annual Union Budget is not passed by the Lok Sabha,",
                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "The answer is (d) — The Prime Minister submits the resignation of the entire Council of Ministers. The Union Budget is a MONEY BILL, and its passage is essentially a VOTE OF CONFIDENCE in the government. If the Lok Sabha rejects the Budget, it means the government no longer has the majority's trust to govern and manage finances. Under the principle of COLLECTIVE RESPONSIBILITY (Article 75): The Council of Ministers is collectively responsible to the Lok Sabha. If the government loses a majority vote on a critical issue like the Budget, the PM must resign along with the entire Council — not just the Finance Minister.\n\nWhy not other options? (a) Budget is not simply 'modified and re-presented' — rejection implies loss of confidence. (b) Rajya Sabha has limited power over Money Bills; it cannot reject them. (c) Only the Finance Minister resigning is insufficient — collective responsibility means the ENTIRE government falls.",
                "option_a": "The Budget is modified and presented again",
                "option_b": "The Budget is referred to the Rajya Sabha for suggestions",
                "option_c": "The Union Finance Minister is asked to resign",
                "option_d": "The Prime Minister submits the resignation of Council of Ministers",
                "pyq_year": "2011",
                "marks": 2,
                "negative_marks": 0.66,
                "formatted_question": "When the annual Union Budget is not passed by the Lok Sabha,",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "The answer is (d) — The Prime Minister submits the resignation of the entire Council of Ministers. \n\nThe Union Budget is a *MONEY BILL*, and its passage is essentially a *VOTE OF CONFIDENCE* in the government. \n\nIf the Lok Sabha rejects the Budget, it means the government no longer has the majority's trust to govern and manage finances. \n\nUnder the principle of *COLLECTIVE RESPONSIBILITY* (Article 75): \n\n- The Council of Ministers is collectively responsible to the Lok Sabha. \n- If the government loses a majority vote on a critical issue like the Budget, the PM must resign along with the entire Council — not just the Finance Minister. \n\nWhy not other options? \n\n(a) Budget is not simply 'modified and re-presented' — rejection implies loss of confidence. \n\n(b) Rajya Sabha has limited power over Money Bills; it cannot reject them. \n\n(c) Only the Finance Minister resigning is insufficient — collective responsibility means the *ENTIRE* government falls."

                "question": "What is the difference between \"vote-on-account\" and \"interim budget\"?\n\n1. The provision of a \"vote-on-account\" is used by a regular Government, while an \"interim budget\" is a provision used by a caretaker Government.\n2. A \"vote-on-account\" only deals with the expenditure in Government's budget, while an \"interim budget\" includes both expenditure and receipts.\n\nWhich of the statements given above is/are correct?",
                "answer": "C",
                "answer_explanation": "Both statements are CORRECT.\n\nStatement 1 (✓): Vote-on-account is typically used by a REGULAR government when the full budget process won't be completed before the new financial year starts (April 1). It gets Parliament's permission to spend money for a few months until the full budget is passed. An interim budget is typically presented by a CARETAKER/OUTGOING government (especially before elections) since the new government may want to present its own full budget. Example: Before the 2019 elections, the Modi government presented an interim budget.\n\nStatement 2 (✓): A vote-on-account deals ONLY with the EXPENDITURE side of the budget — it simply asks Parliament to approve spending for a limited period (usually 2 months). It does NOT deal with revenue proposals (tax changes, new taxes, etc.). An interim budget is a COMPLETE MINI-BUDGET covering BOTH expenditure AND receipts/revenue — it looks like a full budget but is temporary.\n\n**Think of it this way:** Vote-on-account = 'Please let me spend money for 2 months.' Interim budget = 'Here's a complete financial plan until the new government takes over.'",
                "option_a": "1 only",
                "option_b": "2 only",
                "option_c": "Both 1 and 2",
                "option_d": "Neither 1 nor 2",
                "pyq_year": "2011",
                "marks": 2,
                "negative_marks": 0.66,

A \"vote-on-account\" only deals with the expenditure in Government's budget, while an \"interim budget\" includes both expenditure and receipts.  \n\nWhich of the statements given above is/are correct?",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "Both statements are **CORRECT**.\n\nStatement 1 (✓): \n\nVote-on-account is typically used by a **REGULAR** government when the full budget process won't be completed before the new financial year starts (**April 1**). \n\nIt gets Parliament's permission to spend money for a few months until the full budget is passed. \n\nAn interim budget is typically presented by a **CARETAKER/OUTGOING** government (especially before elections) since the new government may want to present its own full budget. \n\nExample: Before the **2019** elections, the **Modi** government presented an interim budget.\n\nStatement 2 (✓): \n\nA vote-on-account deals **ONLY** with the **EXPENDITURE** side of the budget — it simply asks Parliament to approve spending for a limited period (usually **2 months**). \n\nIt does **NOT** deal with revenue proposals (tax changes, new taxes, etc.). \n\nAn interim budget is a **COMPLETE MINI-BUDGET** covering **BOTH** expenditure **AND** receipts/revenue — it looks like a full budget but is temporary.\n\n**Think of it this way:** \n\nVote-on-account = 'Please let me spend money for **2 months**.' \n\nInterim budget = 'Here's a complete financial plan until the new government takes over.'"
